. (We refer to
[
CP98
]
for fundamental facts about
T\!A
.)

Fact 2.5.6

(
[
Hru12
, Proposition 4.7 and Corollary 4.10]
)
.

For
T
as above, the following are equivalent:

(1)

T
has 3-uniqueness.

(2)

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{T}
has 3-existence.

Moreover, assuming in addition that
T\!A
exists, the above conditions imply:

(3)

T\!A
eliminates imaginaries.∎

It is easy to see that if
T\!A
exists and it eliminates bounded hyperimaginaries (
e.g.
, when
T
is superstable, by
[
BPW01
]
combined with
[
CP98
, Corollary 3.8]
), then (3) is actually equivalent to (1) and (2). We will not use this in our paper.

2.5.2.
Twisted independent amalgamation

Let
\tau:\mathcal{L}\cong\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
be a bijection between two first order languages (sending sorts to sorts, function symbols to function symbols consistently with their arity, similarly for constants and relations). Then
\tau
extends naturally to a bijection between the set of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and the set of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-formulas. Given an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
, we denote by
\varphi^{\tau}
its image under this map. If
T
is an
\mathcal{L}
-theory,
T^{\tau}:=\{\varphi^{\tau}:\varphi\in T\}
is an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-theory. Of course, up to changing the names of the symbols using
\tau
,
T^{\tau}
is the
“same”
theory as
T
.

If
M
is an
\mathcal{L}
-structure, we denote by
M^{\tau}
the
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-structure with base set
M
and interpretations
\left(\Sigma^{\tau}\right)^{M^{\tau}}=\Sigma^{M}
, for any symbol
\Sigma\in\mathcal{L}
. If
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-structure, we call an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-isomorphism
\sigma:M^{\tau}\cong N^{\prime}
a
\tau
-twisted isomorphism
between
M
and
N^{\prime}
. Similarly, one defines the notion of a
\tau
-twisted elementary map

\sigma:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
, where
A\subseteq M
and
A^{\prime}\subseteq N^{\prime}
,
i.e.
, one requires that for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
and any tuple
a
from
A
of the right length, one has
M\models\varphi(a)
if and only if
N^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\tau}(\sigma(a))
.

Lemma 2.5.7
.

Let
T
be a complete stable
\mathcal{L}
-theory eliminating quantifiers and imaginaries. Assume that
T
has
n
-uniqueness. Let
A:\mathcal{P}(\bf{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{T}
and
A^{\prime}:\mathcal{P}(\bf{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{T^{\tau}}
be bounded independence preserving functors.

Then for any coherent system
(\sigma_{w})_{w\in\mathcal{P}(\bf{n})^{-}}
of
\tau
-twisted elementary bijections
\sigma_{w}:A(w)\rightarrow A^{\prime}(w)
there exists a
\tau
-twisted elementary bijection
\sigma_{\bf{n}}:A({\bf{n}})\rightarrow A^{\prime}(\bf{n})
extending
\sigma_{w}
for every
w\in\mathcal{P}(\bf{n})^{-}
.

Proof.

The result follows from
n
-uniqueness of
T^{\tau}
, since we may consider
A
as a functor to
\mathcal{C}_{T^{\tau}}
, replacing
A(w)
by
A(w)^{\tau}
.
∎

We now consider the special case where
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}
,
\tau
is a permutation of
\mathcal{L}
and
T
is a complete
\mathcal{L}
-theory such that
T=T^{\tau}
. Let
\widetilde{C}^{(\tau)}_{T}
be the category of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}
-structures

(B,\sigma)
with
B\in\mathcal{C}_{T}
and
\sigma:B\rightarrow B
a
\tau
-twisted elementary bijection. When
T
is stable, we use the same terminology as in
Definition

2.5.5
, for functors
A:P\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
.
Lemma

2.5.7
then yields:

Corollary 2.5.8
.

Let
T
be a complete stable
\mathcal{L}
-theory eliminating quantifiers and imaginaries, and let
\tau:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
be a bijection such that
T^{\tau}=T
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
has 2-existence. If in addition we assume that
T
has 3-uniqueness, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
has 3-existence.

Proof.

Lemma

2.5.7
yields that if
T
has
n
-uniqueness and
n
-existence, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
has
n
-existence. We may thus conclude by
Remark

2.5.4

∎

Given a complete
\mathcal{L}
-theory
T
and a permutation
\tau
of
\mathcal{L}
such that
T^{\tau}=T
, we let
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
be the
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}
-theory whose models are of the form
(M,\sigma)
, where
M\models T
and where
\sigma
is a
\tau
-twisted automorphism of
M
.

Assume now in addition that
T
is stable and eliminates quantifiers and imaginaries. It follows from quantifier elimination in
T
that
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
is then a
\forall\exists
-theory, and so it has a model-companion if and only if the e.c. models of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
form an elementary class. If this is the case, denote by
T^{(\tau)}\!A
the model-companion of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
. Then the models of
T^{(\tau)}\!A
are precisely the e.c. models of of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
. The basic results on
T\!A
, due to Chatzidakis and Pillay (
[
CP98
]
), generalize to this context in a straightforward manner. We will only state some facts which we will need.

Lemma 2.5.9
.

Let
T
and
\tau
be as above, and assume that
T^{(\tau)}\!A
exists. Then the following holds:

(1)

If
(M,\sigma)\models T^{(\tau)}\!A
and
B\subseteq M
then

\mathrm{acl}_{(M,\sigma)}(B)=\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(B):=\mathrm{acl}_{M}(\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma^{z}(B)).

(2)

Quantifier reduction: If
(M_{i},\sigma_{i})\models T^{(\tau)}\!A
and
B_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then
B_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}}B_{2}
if and only if there is an
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}
-isomorphism from
\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(B_{1})
to
\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(B_{2})
sending
B_{1}
to
B_{2}
.

(3)

T^{(\tau)}\!A
is simple and

A\downfree^{T^{(\tau)}\!A}_{E}B\text{ if and only if }\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(EA)\downfree^{T}_{\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(E)}\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(EB).

If
T
is superstable, then
T^{(\tau)}\!A
is supersimple.

(4)

Assume that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}
has 3-existence. (Equivalently, in
T^{(\tau)}\!A
, the independence theorem holds over
\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}
-closed sets.) Then
T^{(\tau)}\!A
eliminates imaginaries.

Proof.

To prove (2), assume that
B=\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(B)
is a common substructure of two models
(M,\sigma)
and
(N,\sigma)
of
T^{(\tau)}\!A
,
such that
(N,\sigma)
is
|M|^{+}
-saturated. We need to show that
(M,\sigma)

\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(B)
-embeds into
(N,\sigma)
. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
has 2-existence, there is an amalgam
(A,\sigma)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
of
N
and
M
over
B
. Enlarging
(A,\sigma)
if necessary, we may assume that
(A,\sigma)\models T^{(\tau)}\!A
, hence
(A,\sigma)\succcurlyeq(N,\sigma)
. In particular,
\mathrm{tp}_{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}}(M/B)
is finitely satisfiable in
(N,\sigma)
, so this type is realized in
(N,\sigma)
by saturation, yielding an
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}(B)
-embedding of
(M,\sigma)
into
(N,\sigma)
.

We now prove (1). Let
(M,\sigma)\models T^{(\tau)}\!A
and let
B\subseteq M
. Clearly,
\mathrm{acl}_{(M,\sigma)}(B)\supseteq\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(B)
. To prove the other inclusion, it suffices to show that if
B=acl_{\sigma}(B)
, then
B
is algebraically closed in
(M,\sigma)
. Let
a\in M\setminus B
, and set
A:=\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(Ba)
. Then
(B,\sigma)\subseteq(A,\sigma)
is an extension in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, using 2-existence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
and induction, we may construct an extension
(B,\sigma)\subseteq(C_{n},\sigma)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
such that
C_{n}
contains
n
isomorphic copies
(A_{1},\sigma),\ldots,(A_{n},\sigma)
of
(A,\sigma)
over
B
, which are
\mathcal{L}
-independent over
B
. Replacing
(M,\sigma)
by an elementary extension if necessary, using (2) we may assume that
C_{n}\subseteq M
. Since
B=\mathrm{acl}_{\mathcal{L}}(B)\mathcal{L}
,
A_{i}\cap A_{j}=B
for any
i\neq j
, so
(M,\sigma)
contains
n
distinct realizations of
\mathrm{tp}_{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}}(a/B)
, by part (2). As
n
was arbitrary,
a\not\in\mathrm{acl}_{(M,\sigma)}(B)
.

To show (3), we proceed exactly as in the proof of the corresponding result for
T\!A
(
[
CP98
, Corollary 3.8]
). If
A,B,E
are subsets of a model of
T^{(\tau)}\!A
, we say that
A
and
B
are
independent
over
E
if
\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(EA)\downfree^{T}_{\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(E)}\mathrm{acl}_{\sigma}(EB)
, where
\downfree^{T}
denotes forking independence in
T
. This relation satisfies the all abstract properties of an independence notion that guarantee, by the Theorem of Kim-Pillay (see,
e.g.
,
[
Wag00
, Theorem 2.6.1]
), that
T^{(\tau)}\!A
is simple and that non-foking is given by the independence notion in question. This is clear for all properties except the independence theorem (over a model). To establish the latter, one shows that every 3-amalgamation problem
A^{-}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{3})^{-}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{(\tau)}_{T}
with
A^{-}(\emptyset)\models T^{(\tau)}\!A

has a solution; equivalently, the independence theorem even holds over models of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
. The proof of this is identical to the proof of
[
CP98
, Theorem 3.7]
.

The statement about supersimplicity follows as in the proof of
[
CP98
, Corollary 3.8]
.

Part (4) is the analog of
[
Hru12
, Proposition 4.7]
. Weak elimination of imaginaries in
T^{(\tau)}\!A
follows directly from a formalization of Hrushovski’s argument by Montenegro and the second author (see
[
MR21
, Proposition 1.17]
). But finite sets are coded in models of
T
, as
T
eliminates imaginaries by assumption. so they are also coded in the expansion
T^{(\tau)}\!A
of
T
. Hence
T^{(\tau)}\!A
eliminates imaginaries.
∎

2.5.3.
Linear imaginaries

Let us now recall some notions from
[
Hru12
, Section 5]
.

Definition 2.5.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be a theory of fields (possibly with additional structure). A
\mathfrak{t}
-linear structure

M
is an
\mathcal{L}
-structure with a sort
\mathbf{k}
for a model of
\mathfrak{t}
, and additional sorts
V_{i}
(
i\in I
)
denoting finite-dimensional
\mathbf{k}
-vector spaces, such that the family
\left(V_{i}\right)_{i\in I}
is closed under tensor products and duals. Each
V_{i}
has (at least) the
\mathbf{k}
-vector space structure. One assumes that
\mathbf{k}
is stably embedded in
M
with induced structure given by
\mathfrak{t}
.

We now fix such a
\mathfrak{t}
-linear structure
M
.

(1)

M
is said to
have flags
if for any
i
with
\dim(V_{i})>1
, for some
j,k
with
\dim(V_{j})=\dim(V_{i})-1
, there exists a
\emptyset
-definable exact sequence
0\longrightarrow V_{k}\longrightarrow V_{i}\longrightarrow V_{j}\longrightarrow 0
. We will call such a short exact sequence a
flag
.

(2)

M
is said to
have roots
if for any one-dimensional
V=V_{i}
, and any
m\geqslant 2
, there
exists a (one-dimensional)
W=V_{j}
and a
\emptyset
-definable
\mathbf{k}
-linear isomorphism
f:W^{\otimes m}\cong V
.

Let us now mention two results from
[
Hru12
]
. The proof of the first one is rather elementary, whereas that of the second one is quite involved.

Fact 2.5.11

(
[
Hru12
, Lemma 5.6]
)
.

The theory of an
\mathrm{ACF}
-linear structure with flags (in any characteristic) eliminates imaginaries.

The following fact follows from
[
Hru12
, Proposition 5.7]
in combination with
[
Hru12
, Proposition 4.3 and Corollary 4.10]
.

Fact 2.5.12
.

Let
T
be the theory of an
\mathrm{ACF}_{0}
-linear structure with flags and roots. Then
T
has 3-uniqueness.

Our main interest in linear structures stems from the fact that the

\mathbf{k}
-internal sets in a given model of
\mathrm{ACVF}
give rise to such a
structure. For every
M\models\mathrm{Hen}_{0}
and
A\subseteq\mathbfcal{G}(M)
, we
define

\mathbf{Lin}_{A}:=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A))\\
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\end{subarray}}s/\ell\mathfrak{m}s.

In equicharacteristic zero, i.e., if
M\models\mathrm{Hen}_{0,0}
, this corresponds
exactly to the collection of vector spaces
\mathrm{VS_{\mathbf{k},A}}=\bigsqcup_{s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A))}s/\mathfrak{m}s
introduced in
[
HHM08
]
.
In mixed characteristic, however, this is a more complicated structure since it
also consists of (free)
\mathbf{R}_{\ell}
-modules — and this more complicated
structure is actually needed in
Section

4
. Note that, by
2.1

and our choice of representation of the geometric sorts,
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
is the
set of cosets
c+\ell\mathfrak{m}s
where
s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A))
has a basis in

M
and
c\in s(M)
.

Lemma 2.5.13
.

Let
M\models\mathrm{Hen}_{0,0}
and
A\subseteq\mathbfcal{G}(M)
. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
, with its
\mathcal{L}_{0}(A)
-induced structure, is an
\mathrm{Th}(\mathbf{k}(M))
-linear structure with flags. Moreover, if
\mathbf{\Gamma}(M)
is divisible, then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
has roots.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A)\cap\mathbfcal{G}(M)\subseteq A
. The fact that the residue field
\mathbf{k}
is stably embedded in
\mathrm{Hen}_{0,0}
, with induced structure that of a pure field, is well known — and follows from the existence of splittings as in
Lemma

2.3.6
.

Now let
V,W
be two sorts from
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
,
i.e.
, vector spaces over
\mathbf{k}
of the form
V=a/\mathfrak{m}a
,
W=b/\mathfrak{m}b
for some
a\in\mathbf{S}_{m}(A)
and
b\in\mathbf{S}_{n}(A)
— with bases in
M
.
Then
a\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}b
is canonically isomorphic to an element
c
from
\mathbf{S}_{m\cdot n}(A)
, so we may identify
V\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}W
with

c/\mathfrak{m}c
, which is a sort from
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
. Similarly,
\check{a}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(a,\mathcal{O})
can be identified with
\{z\in\mathbf{K}^{n}:\forall v\in a,\,\sum z_{i}v_{i}\in\mathcal{O}\}\in\mathbf{S}_{m}(A)
, so
\check{V}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}(V,\mathbf{k})\cong\check{a}/\mathfrak{m}\check{a}
is a sort from
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
as well.

Flags:
For
a\in\mathbf{S}_{n}(A)
define
a_{1}:=a\cap(\mathbf{K}\times\{0\}^{n-1})
. Then the projection onto
the first coordinate identifies
a_{1}
with an element of
\mathbf{S}_{1}(A)
. Let
\pi:a\rightarrow\mathbf{K}^{n-1}
be induced from the projection on the last
n-1
coordinates. Then

0\rightarrow\ker(\pi)=a_{1}\rightarrow a\rightarrow\pi(a)\rightarrow 0

is an
A
-definable
exact sequence of free
\mathcal{O}
-modules, and
\pi(a)\in\mathbf{S}_{n-1}(A)
— this follows from the fact
that
\pi(a)
is a finitely generated torsion free
\mathcal{O}
-submodule of
\mathbf{K}^{n-1}
of rank
n-1
.
Reducing modulo
\mathfrak{m}
, we conclude.

Roots:
Assume
\mathbf{\Gamma}(M)
is divisible. Let
n\geqslant 1
, and let
V
be a one-dimensional sort from
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
.
Then
V=\gamma\mathcal{O}/\gamma\mathfrak{m}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A))
.
Consider
V_{n}:=\delta\mathcal{O}/\delta\mathfrak{m}
, for
\delta=\frac{\gamma}{n}
. The map

x/\gamma\mathfrak{m}\mapsto y/\delta\mathfrak{m}\otimes\ldots\otimes y/\delta\mathfrak{m}:V\rightarrow V_{n}^{\otimes n},

where
y^{n}=x
, is well-defined and an
A
-definable isomorphism of
\mathbf{k}
-vector spaces defined over
A
. In particular,
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
has roots.
∎

The result above actually holds of the
stable part

\bigsqcup_{s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(A))}s/\mathfrak{m}s
in all characteristic (provided
\mathbf{k}
is stably embedded).

Corollary 2.5.14
.

Let
M\models\mathrm{ACVF}_{0,0}
and
A\subseteq\mathbfcal{G}(M)
. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
satisfies 3-uniqueness.∎

2.5.4.
Twisted linear imaginaries

Lemma 2.5.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be a stable theory of fields, and let
T
be the theory of a
\mathfrak{t}
-linear structure such that
T
eliminates quantifiers. Let
\tau
be a permutation of the language with
T=T^{\tau}
such that
\tau
fixes all the formulas on the sort
\mathbf{k}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{t}A
exists. Then
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}\cup\mathfrak{t}A
is the model-companion of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
. In particular, this holds for
\mathfrak{t}=\mathrm{ACF}
.

Proof.

Let
(M,\sigma)\models T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
. Then, as
M\models T
, for any sort
V
from
\mathcal{L}
there is an
M
-definable surjection

f:\mathbf{k}(M)\rightarrow V(M)
. For any
N\succcurlyeq_{\mathcal{L}}M
,
f
then also defines a surjection from
\mathbf{k}(N)
onto
V(N)
, hence
N=\mathrm{dcl}_{\mathcal{L}}(M\mathbf{k}(N))
. Thus, any extension of
\sigma
to a
\tau
-twisted automorphism on
N
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
\mathbf{k}(N)
. It follows that
(M,\sigma)
is an e.c. model of
T^{(\tau)}_{\sigma}
if and only if
(\mathbf{k}(M),{\left.\sigma\right|_{\mathbf{k}(M)}})
is an e.c. model of
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma}
. This yields the statement of the lemma.
∎

As a special case of
Lemma

2.5.15
, we get the following.

Remark 2.5.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be a stable theory of fields, and let
T
be the theory of a
\mathfrak{t}
-linear structure, such that
T
eliminates quantifiers. Suppose
\mathfrak{t}A
exists. Then
T\!A
exists and is given by
T_{\sigma}\cup\mathfrak{t}A
. In particular, this holds for
\mathfrak{t}=\mathrm{ACF}
.

Definition 2.5.17
.

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a stably embedded sort in a theory
T
. An
A_{0}
-definable set
D
is said to be
internally
\mathbf{k}
-internal

(over
A_{0}
), if there is a tuple
d\in D
and an
A_{0}d
-definable surjection
f:Y\rightarrow D
, where
Y\subseteq\mathbf{k}^{n}
for some
n
.

Lemma 2.5.18
.

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a stably embedded sort in a theory
T
, and let
D
be
A_{0}
-definable and internally
\mathbf{k}
-internal (over
A_{0}
). Then
\mathbf{k}\cup D
is stably embedded (over
A_{0}
).

Proof.

Set
D^{\prime}:=\mathbf{k}\cup D
. It follows from the assumptions that
D^{\prime}
is
A_{0}
-definable and internally
\mathbf{k}
-internal over
A_{0}
. Let
f
be an
A_{0}d
-definable surjection as in the definition, with
d\in D^{\prime}
. Taking the preimage under
f\times\cdots\times f
, one sees that any
\mathcal{U}
-definable subset
X
of
D^{\prime m}
is
\mathbf{k}(\mathcal{U})A_{0}d
-definable, by stable embeddedness of
\mathbf{k}
. In particular,
X
is

A_{0}D^{\prime}(\mathcal{U})
-definable, proving stable embeddedness of
D^{\prime}
(over
A_{0}
).
∎

Proposition 2.5.19
.

Let
M\models\mathrm{VFA}^{\mathrm{mult}}_{0,0}
and
A\subseteq\mathbfcal{G}(M)
. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
is stably embedded in
\mathrm{VFA}^{\mathrm{mult}}_{0,0}
and its
A
-induced structure eliminates imaginaries.

Proof.

Stable embeddedness follows from stable embeddedness of
\mathbf{k}
—
cf.

2.4.7
— and the fact that
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
is internally
\mathbf{k}
-internal (by naming a basis for every sort).

Now, let
T
be the theory of
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)
, with its
\mathcal{L}_{0}(A)
-induced structure. By
2.5.11
and
Lemma

2.5.13
,
T
eliminates imaginaries. Let
\tau
be the permutation of
\mathcal{L}_{0}(A)
induced by
\sigma
. Then
\tau
fixes all the formulas on the sort
\mathbf{k}
, and we have
T^{\tau}=T
. It follows from
Corollary

2.5.14
and
Lemma

2.5.9
(4) that
T^{(\tau)}\!A
eliminates imaginaries.

Also, by
2.4.7

(\mathbf{k}(M),\sigma_{\mathbf{k}})
is a stably embedded pure model of
\mathrm{ACFA}
, and hence
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(M)\models T^{(\tau)}\!A
by
Lemma

2.5.15
. Since the
A
-induced structure on
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
is a definable expansion of its
\mathrm{ACF}
-linear structure with a twisted automorphism, elimination of imaginaries follows —
e.g.
, by
[
Hru12
, Lemma 5.4]
.
∎

2.5.5.
Real linear imaginaries

We conclude these preliminaries with a study of
\mathrm{RCF}
-linear structures.

Definition 2.5.20
.

An
\mathrm{RCF}
-linear structure with flags is said to be
oriented
if for
every sort
V
of dimension one, each of the two half lines are

\emptyset
-definable.

Proposition 2.5.21
.

Any oriented
\mathrm{RCF}
-linear structure with flags eliminates imaginaries.

Proof.

Let us first prove a few preliminary results. Let
M
be a sufficiently saturated and homogeneous oriented
\mathrm{RCF}
-linear structure with flags. First, note that if
0\to W\to V\to U\to 0
is an
\emptyset
-definable flag, then any translate of
W
in
V
is ordered by
a<b
if
a-b
is in a fixed half line of
W
.

Claim 2.5.22
.

M
is rigid: for every
A\subseteq M
,
\mathrm{acl}(A)=\mathrm{dcl}(A)
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a non-empty finite
A
-definable set such that all elements of
X
have the same type over
A
. We need to show that
X
is a singleton. Using tensors, we may assume that
X
is contained in some sort
V
. We proceed by induction on
\dim(V)
. Let
0\to W\to V\to U\to 0
be an
\emptyset
-definable flag for
V
. By induction, we may assume that
X
projects to a singleton
b\in U
,
i.e.
,
X
is contained in a translate
a+W
of
W
in
V
and we can conclude since
a+W
inherits an
\emptyset
-definable total order from the ordered group structure on
W
.
∎

Claim 2.5.23
.

Let
X\subseteq c+W\subseteq V
be definable for some
\emptyset
-definable flag
0\to W\to V\to U\to 0
and some
c\in V
. Then
X
is coded.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{k}
is
o
-minimal and there is a definable order preserving bijection between
c+W
and
\mathbf{k}
,
X
is a finite union of points and intervals and hence it is coded by its (finite) border.
∎

Let
K=\mathbf{k}(M)^{\mathrm{a}}
and
K\otimes M
be the structure whose sorts are the sorts
V
of
M
interpreted as
K\otimes_{\mathbf{k}(M)}V(M)
, with the field structure on
\mathbf{k}
, the
\mathbf{k}
-vector space structure on each
V
and the tensor, dual and flag structure. Then
K\otimes M
is an
\mathrm{ACF}
-structure with flags and for every
N\preccurlyeq M
, and tuple
c\in M
, since all of the vector spaces have bases in
N
,
\mathrm{dcl}(Nc)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Nc)
where
\mathrm{acl}_{0}
(respectively
\mathrm{dcl}_{0}
) denotes the algebraic (respectively definable) closure in
K\otimes M
. Note also that in
K\otimes M
, we have
\mathbf{k}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(M))=\mathbf{k}(M)
and since each of the vector spaces has a basis in
M
,
\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(M)\subseteq M
. Since
K\otimes M
eliminates imaginaries by
2.5.11
, it follows that any
M
-definable set in
K\otimes M
has a code consisting of elements in
M
.

By
[
HHM06
, Remark 3.2.2]
, to prove elimination of imaginaries in
M
, it suffices to code every definable function
f:V\to S
, where
S
is a sort. We will proceed by induction on the dimension of
V
.
Let
0\to W\to V\to U\to 0
be an
\emptyset
-definable flag for
V
. Let
F
be the Zariski closure of the graph of
f
in
K\otimes M
— any choice of basis induces a Zariski topology on
V
, but this topology is independent of the choice of coordinates. For every
c\in V(M)
, since
\mathrm{dcl}(Nc)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Nc)
, for every
N\preccurlyeq M
, the fiber
F_{c}
of
F
above
c
is a finite set containing
f(c)
. As was noted above,
F
has a code in
M
. Note that any
\sigma\in\mathrm{Aut}(M/\ulcorner f\urcorner)
can be extended to an automorphism of
K\otimes M
fixing
F
. It follows that
F(M)
is defined over
\ulcorner f\urcorner
and hence it has a code in
M\cap\ulcorner f\urcorner
. Moreover, by compactness and
2.5.22
, in
M
, we find
\ulcorner F\urcorner
-definable maps
(h_{i})_{i<n}
such that for all
c\in V(M)
,
F_{c}(M)=\{h_{i}(a):i<n\}
.

Now, fix
a\in U(M)
and
c\in V
above
a
. Let
f_{a}
be the restriction of
f
to the fiber
c+W
above
a
in
V
. Let
X_{a,i}=\{x\in c+W:f_{a}(x)=h_{i}(x)\}
. By
2.5.23
, this set is coded in
M
. Let
g(a)
denote the tuple consisting of codes of the
X_{i,a}
. The function
g
is
\ulcorner f\urcorner
-definable with domain
U
, so, by induction,
g
is coded in
M
. This concludes the proof since
f
is
(\ulcorner F\urcorner,\ulcorner g\urcorner)
-definable.
∎

Proposition 2.5.24
.

Let
(K,<,v)
be an ordered field with a non-trivial convex valuation and
A\subseteq\mathbfcal{G}(A)
. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(K)
is oriented.

In particular, if
v
is henselian and
\mathbf{k}(K)\models\mathrm{RCF}
, then
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}(K)
is stably embedded and its
A
-induced structure eliminates imaginaries.

Proof.

Dimension one sorts in
\mathbf{Lin}_{A}
are of the form
\gamma\mathcal{O}/\gamma\mathfrak{m}
for some
\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}(K)
. But this quotient inherits the order on
\gamma\mathcal{O}
; so it is oriented. The rest of the proposition follows from
Lemma

2.5.18
,
Proposition

2.5.21
and
Lemma

2.5.13
.
∎

Remark 2.5.25
.

•

Any
K\equiv\mathbb{R}((\mathbb{Q}))
, being real closed, admits a unique field ordering which is definable (without parameters).

•

Any
K\equiv\mathbb{R}((t))
, admits exactly two field orderings, depending on the sign of a choice of uniformizer
\pi
. Both orders are definable (using an imaginary parameter for a half line in
\mathbf{RV}_{1,\mathrm{v}(\pi)}:=\{\xi\in\mathbf{RV}_{1}:\mathrm{v}(\xi)=\mathrm{v}(\pi)\}
, in particular any element of
\mathbf{RV}_{1,\mathrm{v}(\pi)}
).

3.
C
-minimal definable generics

We will now consider generalisations of
[
Rid19
, Theorem 8.7]
. We fix the
following notation for
Sections

3
and
4
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{div}}
and
T_{0}
be the
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-theory
\mathrm{ACVF}
. Let
\mathcal{L}\supseteq\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
T
be a (complete)
\mathcal{L}
-theory of valued fields. Let
M\models T
be sufficiently saturated and homogeneous and
M_{0}=M^{\mathrm{a}}\models T_{0}
. Note that since
\mathrm{ACVF}
eliminates quantifiers, we will implicitly assume that every
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formula is quantifier free. We will denote by
\mathrm{S}^{0}_{x}(M)
the set of (quantifier free)
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-types (in
M_{0}
) in variables
x
and whenever
\Psi(x;t)
is a set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas,
\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{x}(M)
will denote the set of
\Psi
-types over
M
— that is, maximal consistent sets (in
M_{0}
) of formulas
\psi(x;a)
and
\neg\psi(x,a)
with
\psi\in\Psi
and
a\in M^{t}
.

Note that, unless explicitly specified, we do not make any assumption on the
characteristic in
Section

3
.

3.1.
Main results

In this section we prove the following two density results:

Theorem 3.1.1
.

Assume

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{B}}
)

T
is definably spherically complete;

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)

The full induced theory on
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is definably complete;

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})

The full induced theory on
\mathbf{k}
eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
;

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})

The full induced theory on
\mathbf{\Gamma}
eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
.

Then, for every strict pro-
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{x}
, with
x
countable and
A=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}\models T^{\mathrm{eq}}
, there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}_{x}(M)
consistent with
X
.

In other terms, there exists
N\succcurlyeq M
and
a\in X(N)
such that
\mathrm{tp}_{0}(a/M)
is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable. Recall (see,
e.g.
,
[
HL16
, Section 2.2]
) that a set is strict pro-definable if it is the limit of a small directed system of definable sets with surjective transition maps. In other terms, it is a
\star
-definable set whose projection on any finite set of variables is definable.

Proof.

This is a particular case of
Proposition

3.5.1

∎

Remark 3.1.2
.

•

Any (non zero) definably complete ordered abelian group
\Gamma
is
elementarily equivalent to either
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
. Indeed,
\Gamma
cannot
have a proper non trivial definable convex subgroup and is therefore
elementarily equivalent to a subgroup
H
of
(\mathbb{R},+,<)
. If it is not
elementarily equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
,
H
is a dense non divisible
subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
non divisible by

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the cut at
\gamma/n
yields a counter-example.

•

Hypotheses
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{B}}
)
and
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)
are necessary for the conclusion to hold.
Indeed, the conclusion implies that any
\mathfrak{L}(M)
-definable chain
C
of
balls is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable : taking a generic translate, on can ensure that

\bigcap_{b\in C}b
does not contain any
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable chain of
balls, hence any
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable type consistent with this translate of

\bigcap_{b\in C}b
must be the generic of this intersection. Then

\bigcap_{b\in C}b
is a ball, proving both
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{B}}
)
and
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)
.

•

Hypothesis
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
does not allow for discrete value groups. Note however
that the conclusion of the theorem fails in
p
-adic fields. So the hypotheses

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{B}}
)
,
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)
and
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
cannot be sufficient.

•

As
Theorem

3.1.3
illustrates, by restricting to a mild class of
enrichments of
\mathrm{ACVF}_{\forall}
, one can trade hypothesis
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
for purely
algebraic conditions and a weaker conclusion.

Let
\mathrm{Hen}_{0}
be the
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-theory of characteristic zero henselian valued fields.

Theorem 3.1.3

(
cf.

Corollary

3.5.6
)
.

Let
T
be a
\mathbf{k}
-
\mathbf{\Gamma}
-enrichment of
\mathrm{Hen}_{0}
, such that:

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)

The (full) induced theory on
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is definably complete;

(
\mathbf{FR}
)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the interval
[0,\mathrm{v}(n)]
is finite and
\mathbf{k}
is perfect;

(
\mathbf{I}_{\mathbf{k}}
)

The residue field
\mathbf{k}
is infinite;

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})

The (full) induced theory on
\mathbf{k}
eliminates

\exists^{\infty}
.

Then, for every strict pro-
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{x}
, with
A=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}\models T^{\mathrm{eq}}
, there exist an
\mathrm{Aut}(M/\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-invariant
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}_{x}(M)
consistent with
X
.

Note that in this setting
\mathbf{k}
is stably embedded so the full induced structure coincides with the
\emptyset
-induced structure.

Remark 3.1.4
.

•

Contrary to
Theorem

3.1.1
,
Theorem

3.1.3
requires finite ramification in mixed characteristic. Even if
Theorem

3.1.3
does not apply to characteristic zero non-Archimedean local fields either,
cf.
the stronger
[
HMR18
, Remark 4.7]
.

•

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem

3.1.3
, locally, we do find definable types: for any
finite
set
\Psi(x;t)
of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas, we can find an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{x}(M)
consistent with
X
, cf.
Proposition

3.5.4
. This local statement does not hold in characteristic zero non-Archimedean local fields.

•

In both theorems, hypothesis
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
is an artefact of our proof. It is necessary to prove certain intermediate results. However, we do not know if it is necessary to prove either theorems. Moreover, these theorems are the only reason hypothesis
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
appears in the imaginary Ax-Kochen-Ershov principle,
cf.

Theorem

6.1.1
.

Given these observations, the following questions are quite natural:

Question 3.1.5
.

(1)

Can the density of either invariant or definable types —
i.e.
, the conclusion of either theorem — be proved without assuming
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
?

(2)

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem

3.1.3
, can we find an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable type
p
?

(3)

Can the hypotheses of
Theorem

3.1.3
be weakened to also encompass characteristic zero non-Archimedean local fields?

3.2.
The uniform arity one case

We start by giving a succinct (and slightly more general) presentation of
terminology and results from
[
Rid19
, Section 6 and 7]
.
The types in
Theorems

3.1.3
and
3.1.1
are found by finding, for every unary set,
a close-fitting intersection of balls (uniformly over realizations of a type
found at an earlier step). To obtain anything definable, we need to localize to
definable families of (finite sets of) balls. The main technical issue is then
to find large enough families such that the approximation (and later induction
on arity) goes through while keeping it small enough that it stays definable;
this is achieved with the notion of good presentation (
Definition

3.2.7
).

Once these have been introduced, the main goal of this section is to give two
versions of the approximation process (
Corollaries

3.2.18
and
3.2.21
). We invite a first
reader to assume in
Definition

3.2.15
and onwards that
p
is an arity zero type
(equivalently a realized type) and that
F_{\lambda}
is the collection of all
balls (open and closed) to get an idea of the base arity one case with fixed
parameters.

Definition 3.2.1
.

We define
\mathbf{B}
to be the (
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable) set of balls (closed or
open) in models of
T_{0}
— the field itself is the open ball of radius

-\infty
and points are closed balls of radius
+\infty
. For every

r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
is the set of finite (potentially empty)
subsets of cardinality at most
r+1
of
\mathbf{B}
of the same radius
and either all open or all closed — in particular, there is no nesting among
the elements of some
B\in{r}
. Let also
\mathbf{B}^{[<\infty]}:=\bigcup_{r>0}\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
.

Similarly, we denote
\mathbf{K}^{[r]}\subseteq\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
the set of finite
subsets of cardinality at most
r+1
of
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{K}^{[<\infty]}:=\bigcup_{r>0}\mathbf{K}^{[r]}\subseteq\mathbf{B}^{[<\infty]}
.

For every finite set of balls
B
, we define
B^{\cup}:=\bigcup_{b\in B}b
and for every finite sets of balls
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, we write
B_{1}\leqslant B_{2}
if
B_{1}^{\cup}\subseteq B_{2}^{\cup}
. For every

b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbf{B}
, we also define
d(b_{1},b_{2}):=\inf\{\mathrm{v}(x_{1}-x_{2}):x_{i}\in b_{i}\}
. Note that this is not a metric on the space of balls since

d(b_{1},b_{1})=\mathrm{rad}(b_{1})
, the radius of
b_{1}
. Finally, for

B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
, we define
D(B_{1},B_{2}):=\{d(b_{1},b_{2}):b_{i}\in B_{i}\}
. We enumerate
D(B_{1},B_{2})\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
in increasing order. Let

d_{i}(B_{1},B_{2})
be the
i
-th element of this enumeration. So that

d_{i}(B_{1},B_{2})
is defined for all
i<r^{2}
, for every
i
above the
cardinality of
D(B_{1},B_{2})
, we set
d_{i}(B_{1},B_{2})
to the the maximal
element. This choice of an enumeration (with repetitions) of
D(B_{1},B_{2})
does
not actually matter, as long as it is chosen uniformly.

Let us now fix a set
\Psi(x;t)
of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas, an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
set
\Lambda
, an integer
r
and an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable family
F=(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of functions
F_{\lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
. We now wish to give sufficient conditions on
\Psi
and
F

that will allow us to proceed with certain classical unary constructions in
valued fields, uniformly over realisations of
\Psi
-types. In particular, it
will allow is to describe (local) types in
n+1
-variables as generics of
balls parametrized by
n
-variables.

Definition 3.2.2
.

Let
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{x}(M)
.

(1)

We say that
p
is adapted to
F
if, for each of the following
statement,
p
implies either this statement or its negation:

•

F_{\lambda}(x)\square\bigcup_{i<r}F_{\mu_{i}}(x)
where
\lambda,\mu_{i}\in\Lambda(M)
and
\square\in\{=,\subseteq,\subset,\leqslant,<\}
;

•

F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)=F_{\mu_{1}}^{\cup}(x)\cap F_{\mu_{2}}^{\cup}(x)
, where
\lambda,\mu_{i}\in\Lambda(M)
;

•

Every ball in
F_{\lambda}(x)
is closed;

•

\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))\square d_{i}(F_{\mu_{1}}(x),F_{\mu_{2}}(x))
, where
\lambda,\mu_{i}\in\Lambda(M)
,
\square\in\{=,\leqslant\}
and
i<r^{2}
;

(2)

We say that
F
is closed under intersections over
p
if for every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(M)
, there exists
\epsilon\in\Lambda(M)
with
p(x)\vdash F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\cap F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)=F_{\epsilon}^{\cup}(x)
— and we further assume that there exists
\eta\in\Lambda(M)
such that
F_{\eta}(x)=\{\mathbf{K}\}
.

(3)

We say that
F
is closed under complement over
p
if for every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(M)
, with
p(x)\vdash F_{\mu}(x)\subseteq F_{\lambda}(x)
, there exists
\epsilon\in\Lambda(M)
with
p(x)\vdash F_{\epsilon}(x)=F_{\lambda}(x)\setminus F_{\mu}(x)
.

Note that, in the above definition, the
\mathfrak{L}
-structure on
M
does not matter.

Remark 3.2.3
.

The family of all constant functions to
\mathbf{B}
(over any type), is an
important example of the above properties. This simple family suffices to prove

Theorems

3.1.1
and
3.1.3
for
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{1}
. Dealing with higher arity
definable sets, however, requires non-constant functions.

Let now
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{x}(M)
be adapted to
F
, and let us assume that

F
is closed under intersections and complement over
p
.

Definition 3.2.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
. We say that
F_{\lambda}
is
irreducible over
p
, if for every
\mu\in\Lambda(M)
,
p(x)\vdash F_{\mu}(x)\subseteq F_{\lambda}(x)
implies
p(x)\vdash F_{\mu}(x)=\emptyset\vee F_{\mu}(x)=F_{\lambda}(x)
.

We define
\Lambda_{p}(M):=\{\lambda\in\Lambda(M):F_{\lambda}\text{ irreducible over }p\}
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
, there exists finitely many

\mu_{i}\in\Lambda_{p}(M)
with
p(x)\vdash F_{\lambda}(x)=\bigcup_{i}F_{\mu_{i}}(x)
.

Proof.

Since
p
is adapted to
F
it suffices to check that the lemma holds at one
realization
a
of
p
. We can then proceed by induction on the cardinality
of
F_{\lambda}(a)
, using closure under complement to conclude.
∎

Lemma 3.2.6
.

For every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda_{p}(M)
, we have

p(x)\vdash F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\cap F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)=\emptyset\vee F_{\lambda}(x)\leqslant F_{\mu}(x)\vee F_{\mu}(x)\leqslant F_{\lambda}(x).

Proof.

Again, it suffices to check for one
a\models p
. We may assume that the balls
in
F_{\lambda}(a)
have smaller (or equal) radius than those in
F_{\mu}(a)

and if the radii are equal and the balls in
F_{\lambda}(a)
are open, so are
the balls in
F_{\mu}(a)
. By closure under intersection, we find

\epsilon\in\Lambda(M)
such that
F^{\cup}_{\lambda}(a)\cap F_{\mu}^{\cup}(a)=F_{\epsilon}^{\cup}(a)
. By hypothesis on the radii,

F_{\epsilon}(a)\subseteq F_{\lambda}(a)
and hence, by irreducibility, either

F_{\epsilon}(a)=\emptyset
or
F_{\epsilon}(a)=F_{\lambda}(a)
.
∎

We will later need some further hypotheses (
cf.

Lemma

3.3.1
) on
\Psi
and
F
leading to the following definition:

Definition 3.2.7
.

Let
\Psi(x;t)
be a set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas, and
F=(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
be a definable family of functions — for some
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
\Lambda
and integer
r
. We say that
(\Psi,F)
is a good presentation if, for any
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{x}(M)

(1)

p
is adapted to
F
;

(2)

F
is closed under intersection and complement over
p
;

(3)

F
has large balls over
p
,
i.e.
, for every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(M)
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
:

•

If
p(x)\vdash F_{\lambda}(x)\neq\mathbf{K}
, there is
\eta\in\Lambda(M)
such that
p(x)\vdash\mathrm{rad}(F_{\eta}(x))=d_{i}(F_{\lambda}(x),F_{\mu}(x))\wedge{}
“
F_{\eta}(x)
is closed”
{}\wedge F_{\lambda}(x)\leqslant F_{\eta}(x)
;

•

If
p(x)\vdash

“
F_{\lambda}(x)
is open”
\,\vee\,\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))<d_{i}(F_{\lambda}(x),F_{\mu}(x))
, there is

\eta\in\Lambda(M)
such that,
p(x)\vdash\mathrm{rad}(F_{\eta}(x))=d_{i}(F_{\lambda}(x),F_{\mu}(x))\wedge{}
“
F_{\eta}(x)
is open”
{}\wedge F_{\lambda}(x)\leqslant F_{\eta}(x)
.

Let
\Delta(xy;s)
with
|y|=1
be a set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas. We say
that
(\Psi,F)
is a
good presentation for
\Delta
if
(\Psi,F)

is a good presentation and every
M
-instance of
\Delta
is a Boolean
combination of
M
-instances of
\Psi
and formulas

y\in F_{\lambda}(x)^{\cup}
with
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
.

Let
G:=(G_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Omega}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[\ell]}
be an

\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable family of functions. If, moreover, for every

\omega\in\Omega(M)
, there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
such that

G_{\omega}=F_{\lambda}
. we say that
(\Psi,F)
is a
good presentation
for
(\Delta,G)
.

An important point is that
finite
good presentations always exist:

Proposition 3.2.8
.

Let
\Delta(xy;s)
be a finite set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas with
|y|=1
and
(G_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Omega}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[\ell]}
be
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable. Then there exists a finite set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas
\Psi(x;t)
and an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
F:=(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
such that
(\Psi,F)
is a good presentation for
(\Delta,G)
.

We only sketch the proof — details of the precise encodings can be found in
[
Rid19
, Proposition 6.14, 6.15, 6.18 and 7.12]
.

Proof.

The existence of
\Psi
and
F
such that any instance of
\Delta
is an Boolean combination of instances of
\Psi
and
y\in F^{\cup}_{\lambda}(x)
follows by compactness from the Swiss cheese decomposition. Enlarging
F
, we may assume it contains
G
and that condition (3) hold. At any point, enlarging
\Psi
, we may assume that condition (1) holds. Since the intersection of two balls is either empty or one of these balls,
F
can be closed under intersection by considering the family of
r+1
-fold intersections of
F
. Closure under complement can be obtained by considering the finite Boolean algebra generated by the subsets, appearing in
F
, of any given
F_{\lambda}
(over some realisation of
p
). They are generated in (uniformly) finitely many steps and hence can be considered as the elements of one single family. This concludes the proof since the previous two steps preserve condition (3).
∎

Remark 3.2.9
.

A good presentation
(\Psi(x;t),F)
remains a good presentation as
\Psi
grows. So, given a set
\Psi(xy;t)
of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas and an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
F:=(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
, we say that
(\Psi(xy;t),F)
is a good presentation if there exists
\Phi(x;t)\subseteq\Psi
such that
(\Phi(x;t),F)
is a good presentation.

We now fix a good presentation
(\Psi(xy;t),F)
, with
|y|=1
, and
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{xy}(M)
. Let
(\Psi,F)
be the set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas
\Psi\cup\{y\in F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\}
in variables
xy
and parameters
t\lambda
. Let
\mathrm{S}^{\Psi,F}_{xy}(M)
denote the space of
(\Psi,F)
-types over
M
(in
M_{0}
).

Definition 3.2.10
.

For every
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable maps
f,g:X\to Y
and every partial

\mathcal{L}(M)
-type
q
concentrating on
X
, we say that
f
and
g
have
the same
q
-germ
, and we write
[f]_{q}=[g]_{q}
, if

q(x)\vdash f(x)=g(x)
.

Definition 3.2.11
.

For every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(M)
, we write
\lambda\leqslant_{p}\mu
whenever

p(xy)\vdash F_{\lambda}(x)\leqslant F_{\mu}(x).

Note that
\leqslant_{p}
is an
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable pre-order. Recall that elements
of any
B\in\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
cannot be nested. It follows that, for any two

B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
,
B_{1}=B_{2}
if and only if
B_{1}^{\cup}=B_{2}^{\cup}
. So the equivalence relation associated to
\leqslant_{p}
is equality
of
p
-germs. We therefore write
\lambda<_{p}\mu
whenever
\lambda\leqslant_{p}\mu
and they have distinct
p
-germs.

Moreover, when restricted to
\Lambda_{p}
, by
Lemma

3.2.6
,
there is a largest element,
\mathbf{K}
, and the
\leqslant_{p}
-upwards closure of any

\lambda\in\Lambda_{p}\smallsetminus[\emptyset]_{p}
is totally ordered:

(\Lambda_{p}\smallsetminus[\emptyset]_{p},\leqslant_{p})
is a tree.

Definition 3.2.12
.

Let
E\subseteq\Lambda_{p}(M)
. The
generic type of
E
above
p
is

\displaystyle\eta_{E,p}(xy)

\displaystyle:=p(xy)

\displaystyle\cup\{y\in F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x):\mu\in E\}

\displaystyle\cup\{y\nin F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x):\lambda\in\Lambda(M)\wedge\forall\mu\in E\ \lambda<_{p}\mu\}.

This is the partial type of realizations of
p
such that
y
is in

\bigcap_{\mu\in E}F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)
, but in no strict subset of the form

F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)
. Provided
\eta_{E,p}
is consistent it generates a
complete
(\Psi,F)
-type that we also denote

\eta_{E,p}\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi,F}_{xy}(M)
. Further assuming that
p
is

\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable (as a
\Psi
-type), then
\Lambda_{p}(M)
is a

\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable set, and if
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Lambda_{p}
is an

\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable then the type
\eta_{\mathcal{E}(M),p}
is an

\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable
(\Psi,F)
-type, provided it is consistent. We denote it

\eta_{\mathcal{E},p}
.

Definition 3.2.13
.

Let
\pi(x)
be a partial
\mathcal{L}(M)
-type and
A\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}
. We say
that
\pi
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
if, for every

\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x;t)
, there exists an
\mathcal{L}(A)
-formula
\theta(t)

such that
\{b\in M^{t}:\pi(x)\vdash\varphi(x;b)\}=\theta(M)
. When it
exists, we write
\forall_{\!\pi}x\,\varphi(x;t):=\theta(t)
and
\exists_{\!\pi}x\,\varphi(x;t)=\neg(\forall_{\!p}x\,\neg\varphi(x;t))
.

Remark 3.2.14
.

(1)

Such a type is often also a
“definable partial type”
in the literature.
There is however some ambiguity on the terminology,
cf.

[
Rid19
, Remark 7.2.(ii)]
, hence the present distinct choice of
terminology.

(2)

If, for some set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas
\Delta(x;y)
,
p(x)
is a
complete
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable
\Delta
-type over
M
, then it is

\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable, as a
\Delta
-type — that is
\forall_{p}x\,\varphi(x;t)
exists for every
\varphi(x;y)\in\Delta
. As we will see in

Lemma

3.3.1
, under certain hypotheses on
T
and
\Delta
, the converse
also holds.

We can now prove the crucial step in proving
Theorems

3.1.1
and
3.1.3
: the relative arity one case. Let us now assume that
p
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
, where
A=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}
, and consistent with some
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{xy}
.

Definition 3.2.15
.

For every
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda_{p}
, let
\lambda\trianglelefteqslant\mu
hold whenever

\forall_{\!p}{xy}\,y\in(X_{x}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x))\to y\in F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x),

where
X_{x}=\{y:xy\in X\}
denotes the fiber above
x
.

The relation
\trianglelefteqslant
is an
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable preorder on
\Lambda_{p}
and
we denote
\equiv
the associated equivalence relation. Since
\leqslant_{p}

refines
\trianglelefteqslant
on
\Theta_{p}:=\Lambda_{p}\smallsetminus(\emptyset/{\equiv})
,
this is also a tree with root
\mathbf{K}/\equiv
and
\equiv
-classes are

\leqslant_{p}
-convex.

Lemma 3.2.16
.

For every
\lambda\in\Theta_{p}
, if the generic

\eta_{\lambda/{\equiv},p}
of
\lambda/{\equiv}
over
p
is not
consistent with
X
, then
\lambda/{\equiv}
has finitely many

\trianglelefteqslant
-daughters
(\mu_{i}/{\equiv})_{0\leqslant i<n}\in\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A\ulcorner\lambda/{\equiv}\urcorner)
in
\Theta_{p}/{\equiv}
. Moreover,

n\geqslant 2
and
p(xy)\vdash y\in X_{x}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\to\bigvee_{i<n}y\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
.

Proof.

Let us assume that
\eta_{\lambda/{\equiv},p}
is not consistent with

X
,
i.e.

p(xy)\cup\{(x,y)\in X\}\cup\{y\in F_{\lambda}(x)\}\cup\{y\nin F_{\nu}(x):\nu\triangleleft\lambda\}
is not consistent. By compactness,
there exist
(\nu_{i})_{0\leqslant i<m}\in\Theta_{p}(M)
such that
\nu_{i}\triangleleft\lambda
and
p(xy)\vdash y\in X_{x}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\to\bigvee_{i<m}y\in F_{\nu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
. The existence of the
\mu_{i}
now follows from
the facts that any
\mu\trianglelefteqslant\lambda
is
\trianglelefteqslant
-comparable to one of the

\nu_{i}
and that since the
F_{\nu_{i}}
are irreducible, the subtree with
root
\lambda
and leaves
(\nu_{i})_{i<m}
embeds in the lattice of subsets
of
\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
, which is finite — we refer the reader to

[
Rid19
, Claim 8.4]
for details. Finally, if
n=1
, we would have

\lambda\trianglelefteqslant\mu_{0}
, contradicting that
\mu_{0}
is a daughter of

\lambda
.
∎

Let
\overline{\Theta}_{p}:=\{\mu\in\Theta_{p}:\forall_{\!p}xy

“
F_{\mu}(x)
is
closed”
\}
and, for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
, let
Y_{\lambda}:=\{\mu\in\overline{\Theta}_{p}:\forall_{\!p}xy\,\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu}(x))=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))\}
.

Lemma 3.2.17
.

One of the following holds:

•

There exists a
\lambda\in\Theta_{p}
such that
\lambda/{\equiv}\in A
and
\eta_{\lambda/\equiv,p}
is consistent with
X
.

•

There exists
\lambda/{\equiv}\in A
with
[Y_{\lambda}]_{p}:=\{[F_{\mu}]_{p}:\mu\in Y_{\lambda}\}
finite of arbitrarily large
cardinality.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is consistent with no
\eta_{\lambda/{\equiv},p}
, where
\lambda/{\equiv}\in A
. Then, by
Lemma

3.2.16
,
\Theta_{p}/\equiv
admits an initial finitely strictly branching discrete tree — that is every, element has at least two daughters — with every branch infinite. Note that, for every
\lambda\in\Theta_{p}
with
\lambda<_{p}\mathbf{K}
, by the large ball property, there is
\mu\in\Lambda
with
\lambda\leqslant_{p}\mu
and
\forall_{\!p}{xy}

“
F_{\mu}(x)
is closed”
{}\wedge\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu}(x))=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))
. We may assume that
F_{\mu}
is irreducible over
p
. Then
\lambda=\mu
or
\lambda
is the unique
\leqslant_{p}
-daugther of
\mu
. Note also that, by the large ball property,
\overline{\Theta}_{p}\cap\mathbf{K}/{\equiv}\neq\emptyset
. It follows that
\overline{\Theta}_{p}/\equiv
also admits an initial finitely branching discrete tree, denoted
\Xi_{p}
, with every branch infinite.

Note that, for any two
\mu,\nu\in Y_{\lambda}
, since
\forall_{\!p}xy\,\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu}(x))=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\nu}(x))
, we have that
[F_{\mu}]_{p}=[F_{\nu}]_{p}
implies
\mu\equiv\nu
, which implies that
\forall_{\!p}{xy}\,F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)\cap F_{\nu}^{\cup}(x)\neq\emptyset
, which, by irreducibility, implies that
[F_{\mu}]_{p}=[F_{\nu}]_{p}
, so these three statements are equivalent. In particular, the identity induces a bijection between
[Y_{\lambda}]_{p}
and
Y_{\lambda}/{\equiv}
.

We now build, by induction,
\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
such that
Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}\subseteq\Xi_{p}
and
|Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}|=|[Y_{\lambda_{i}}]_{p}|
is finite and strictly increasing. Start with any
\lambda_{0}\in\overline{\Theta}_{p}\cap\mathbf{K}/{\equiv}
. Then
Y_{\lambda_{0}}=[F_{\lambda}]_{p}
and
Y_{\lambda_{0}}/{\equiv}=\lambda_{0}/{\equiv}=\mathbf{K}/{\equiv}
. If
\lambda_{i}
is built, let
(\mu_{j})_{j<m}
enumerate all the
\trianglelefteqslant
-daughters — in
\Xi_{p}
— of the elements in
Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}
. Let
j_{0}
be such that, for all
j
,
\forall_{\!p}{xy}\,\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu_{j_{0}}}(x))\leqslant\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu_{j}}(x))
. For every
\nu\in Y_{\mu_{j_{0}}}
, by the large ball property, we find
\lambda\in Y_{\lambda_{i}}
such that
\nu\leqslant_{p}\lambda
. Since
\forall_{\!p}{xy}\ \mathrm{rad}(F_{\nu}(x))=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu_{j_{0}}}(x))\leqslant\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu_{j}}(x))
, we cannot have
\nu\triangleleft\mu_{j}
and hence
\nu/{\equiv}
is either in
Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}
or it is one of the
\mu_{j}/{\equiv}
. So
Y_{\mu_{j_{0}}}/{\equiv}\subseteq\Xi_{p}
is finite.

Furthermore, for every
\mu_{j}
, by the large ball property, there exists
\nu\in Y_{\mu_{j_{0}}}
such that
\mu_{j}\leqslant_{p}\nu
. It follows that, for every element of
Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}
, either it or all of its (more than one) daughters appear in
Y_{\mu_{j_{0}}}
. In particular, all the sisters of
\mu_{j_{0}}/{\equiv}
appear, and hence
|Y_{\mu_{j_{0}}}/{\equiv}|>|Y_{\lambda_{i}}/{\equiv}|
. Thus, we can choose
\lambda_{i+1}=\mu_{j_{0}}
.
∎

We can now eliminate the second option in
Lemma

3.2.17
by imposing a uniform bound on the size of finite instances of
(Y_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
:

Corollary 3.2.18
.

Assume:

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\overline{F}}
)

For every
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable family
(Y_{z})_{z}
of subsets of
[F_{\Lambda_{p}}]_{p}:=\{[F_{\lambda}]_{p}:\lambda\in\Lambda_{p}\}
such that for all
z
and
[F_{\lambda}]_{p},[F_{\mu}]_{p}\in Y_{z}
,
p(xy)\vdash

“
F_{\mu}(x)
is closed”
{}\wedge\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\mu}(x))
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that, for all
z
,
|Y_{z}|<\infty
implies
|Y_{z}|\leqslant n
.

Then there exists an
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Lambda_{p}
such that
\eta_{\mathcal{E},p}
is consistent with
X
.∎

However the family
[Y_{\Lambda}]_{p}
is not any definable family in

[F_{\Lambda_{p}}]_{p}
. It has certain geometric properties that reflect
that of
X
. In particular, with further hypotheses on
X
, we can dispense
with
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\overline{F}}
)
altogether, as we will see in
Lemma

3.2.21
.

We now wish to apply the construction above in the pair
(M_{0},M)
which is naturally an
\mathcal{L}_{0,P}
structure enriched with the
\mathcal{L}
-structure on
M
. To be precise and avoid an unnecessary conflict of notation:

Notation 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
be some expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
T_{1}
some
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-theory of valued fields. In the following lemma, we apply the above with
T
the theory of the pair
M:=(M_{1}^{\mathrm{a}},M_{1})
, where
M_{1}\models T_{1}
is sufficiently saturated and homogeneous, in the language
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{P}}
consisting of
\mathcal{L}_{0,P}
structure enriched with the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-structure on
\mathbf{P}
— so
M_{0}=M_{1}^{\mathrm{a}}
.

Let us now introduce some useful terminology from
[
CHR19
]
:

Definition 3.2.19
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
invertible in

\mathbf{K}(M)
. For any ball
b
, we define
b[n]:=\{a+n^{-1}(a-c):a,c\in b\}
. It is a ball of radius
\mathrm{rad}(b)-\mathrm{v}(n)
around
b
, open if

b
is, closed otherwise. For a set of balls
B
, we set
B[n]:=\{b[n]:b\in B\}
.

(1)

An
\mathcal{L}(M_{1})
-definable set
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}(M_{1})
is

n
-prepared
by some finite set
C\subseteq\mathbf{K}(M_{0})
if for every ball

b\in\mathbf{B}(M_{0})
with
b[n]\cap C=\emptyset
, either
b\cap X(M_{1})=\emptyset
or
b\cap X(M_{1})=b(M_{1})
.

(2)

We say that some
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M_{1})
-definable
G:\mathbf{K}^{n}\to\mathbf{K}^{[r]}

n
-prepares

X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{n+1}(M_{1})
if, for every

x\in\mathbf{K}(M_{1})^{n}
,
G(x)

n
-prepares
X_{x}
.

(3)

We say that
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{n+1}(M_{1})
is
n
-prepared
by
F

if there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda(M_{1})
such that
F_{\lambda}
has values in

\mathbf{K}^{[r]}
and
n
-prepares
X
.

Remark 3.2.20
.

By field quantifier elimination (
cf.

2.2.7
), if
M_{1}
is a pure henselian field of characteristic zero, any
\mathcal{L}(M_{1})
-definable
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}
is
p^{\ell}
-prepared, for some
\ell
, by the finite set of roots of polynomials that appear in the (field quantifier free) definition of
X
, where
p
is either
1
or the residue characteristic when it is positive.

Let also
A_{1}=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{1}(A_{1})\subseteq M_{1}^{\mathrm{eq}}
,
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{xy}(M_{1})

be
\mathcal{L}_{1}(A_{1})
-definable,
A=A_{P}=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A_{1})
and

p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{xy}(M_{0})
be
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{P}}(A_{P})
-definable and consistent with

X
.

Lemma 3.2.21
.

Let
\Phi(x;t)\subseteq\Psi
be such that
(\Phi,F)
is a good presentation for
\Psi
. Assume that there is some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cap\mathbf{K}^{\times}(M)
such that:

(
\mathbf{P}^{F,n}_{X}
)

The set
X
is
n
-prepared by
F
;

(
\mathbf{FR}_{n}
)

The interval
[0,\mathrm{v}(n)]\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}(M_{1})
is finite and
\mathbf{k}(M_{1})
is perfect;

(
\mathbf{I}_{\mathbf{k}}
)

The residue field
\mathbf{k}(M_{1})
is infinite.

Then, there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{P}}(A_{P})
-definable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Lambda_{p}(M_{0})
such that
{\left.\eta_{\mathcal{E},p}\right|_{M_{0}}}
is consistent with
X
.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be such that
F_{\rho}(x)

n
-prepares
X_{x}
for all
x
. By
Lemma

3.2.17
, applied in
M=(M_{1}^{\mathrm{a}},M_{1})
, either the conclusion of the
Lemma
holds, or we can find
\lambda/{\equiv}\in A
with
|[Y_{\lambda}]_{p}|>r
. Then some element of
Y_{\lambda}
, say
F_{\lambda}
, does not contain any point of
F_{\rho}(x)
. Replacing
\lambda
with any
\mu\trianglelefteqslant\lambda
in
\Xi_{p}
such that
|[\mu,\lambda]|\geqslant|[0,\mathrm{v}(n)]|
, we may further assume that
F_{\rho}(x)\cap F_{\lambda}(x)[n]^{\cup}=\emptyset
— where, for any
B\in\mathbf{B}^{[<\infty]}
,
B[n]:=\{b[n]:b\in B\}
— and that
\lambda\triangleleft\mathbf{K}
.

By
Lemma

3.2.16
, we have
p(xy)\vdash y\in X_{x}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\to\bigvee_{i<n}y\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
, where the
(\mu_{i}/{\equiv})_{i<n}
are the daughters of
\lambda/{\equiv}
. By compactness, there exists some
\psi(xy)\in p
such that
q:={\left.p\right|_{\Phi}}\vdash\forall y\ \psi(xy)\wedge y\in X_{x}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\to\bigvee_{i<n}y\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
. Since
(\Phi,F)
is a good presentation for
\Psi
, there are
\kappa
and
(\mu_{i})_{n\leqslant i<m}\in\Lambda_{p}
with
\models\psi(xy)\leftrightarrow y\in F_{\kappa}^{\cup}(x)\smallsetminus(\bigcup_{n\leqslant i<m}F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x))
. In particular,
p(xy)\vdash y\in F_{\kappa}^{\cup}(x)
. It follows that
\kappa\equiv\mathbf{K}
and hence
\lambda\trianglelefteqslant\kappa
. So, we have

q\vdash F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\cap X_{x}\subseteq\bigcup_{i}F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x).

Since
\lambda\nequiv\emptyset
, there exists
ac\models p
in some
N_{1}\succcurlyeq M_{1}
such that
c\in X_{a}\cap F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(a)
. Let
b_{0}\in\mathbf{B}(N_{1}^{\mathrm{a}})
be the ball of
F_{\lambda}(a)
containing
c
. Since
F_{\rho}(a)\cap b_{0}[n]=\emptyset
, we have
b_{0}\cap X_{a}(N_{1})=b_{0}(N_{1})
. It follows that
b_{0}(N_{1})\subseteq\bigcup_{i}F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(a)
. By construction,
b_{0}(N_{1})
is not covered by any single ball in
\bigcup_{i}F_{\mu_{i}}(a)
. So
\{\mathrm{v}(x-y):x,y\in b_{0}(N_{1})\}
has a minimal element (realised by some
x,y
in distinct balls of
\bigcup_{i}F_{\mu_{i}}(a)
). Let
b\in\mathbf{B}(N_{1})
be the smallest closed ball containing
b_{0}(N_{1})
. Then
b(N_{1})=b_{0}(N_{1})
is covered by finitely many of its maximal open subballs, contradicting hypothesis
(
\mathbf{I}_{\mathbf{k}}
)
.
∎

3.3.
Quantifiable types

To use the above constructions in an induction, we need a number of results on
quantifiable types. The first one is that
\eta_{\mathcal{E},p}
is itself
quantifiable when
p
is. Recall our general setup (
3.1
) for this
section.

For any finite set
B
of balls, let
\mathbf{k}_{B}
be
the set of maximal open subballs of the balls
b\in B
and
\mathrm{res}_{B}:B^{\cup}\to\mathbf{k}_{B}
be the projection.

Lemma 3.3.1

(cf.
[
Rid19
, Corollary 6.9]
)
.

Let
(\Psi(xy;t),(F_{\lambda}(x))_{\lambda\in\Lambda})
be a good presentation. Let
p\in\mathrm{S}^{\Psi}_{xy}(M)
be
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
, where
A\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}
. Assume:

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\mathring{F}}
)

For any
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable
(Y_{z})_{z}\subseteq[F_{\Lambda}]_{p}
and
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
such that, for all
z
and
[F_{\mu}]_{p}\in Y_{z}
,
p(xy)\vdash F_{\mu}(x)\subseteq\mathbf{k}_{F_{\lambda}(x)}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that, for all
z
,
|Y_{z}|<\infty
implies
|Y_{z}|\leqslant n
.

Then, any
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
q\in\mathrm{S}_{xy}^{\Psi,F}(M)
containing
p
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{p}:q(xy)\vdash y\in F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\}
. Then
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable and
q=\eta_{\mathcal{E},p}
. If
\mathcal{E}
does not have an
\leqslant_{p}
-minimal element which is closed, for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(xy;s)
and
e\in M^{s}
,
q(xy)\vdash\varphi(xy;e)
if and only if there exists
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(M)
and
\mu\in\Lambda_{p}(M)
with
\lambda<_{p}\mathcal{E}
and
q(xy)\vdash y\in F_{\lambda}^{\cup}(x)\smallsetminus F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)\to\varphi(xy;e)
— cf.
[
Rid19
, Proposition 6.4]
. So let us assume that
\mathcal{E}
has an
\leqslant_{p}
-minimal element
\lambda_{0}
which consists of closed balls. If
p(xy)\vdash\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda_{0}}(x))=+\infty
, then
q(xy)\vdash\varphi(xy;e)
if and only if
p(xy)\vdash F_{\lambda_{0}}^{\cup}(x)\to\varphi(xy;e)
— cf.
[
Rid19
, Proposition 6.6]
. If
p(xy)\vdash\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda_{0}}(x))\neq+\infty
, let

Y_{s}:=\{\mu\in\Lambda_{p}:\forall_{\!p}xy\ F_{\mu}(x)\subseteq\mathbf{k}_{F_{\lambda}(x)}\wedge\exists_{\!p}xy\ \varphi(xy;s)\wedge y\in F_{\mu}^{\cup}(x)\}.

Let
n
be a uniform bound on the cardinality of finite
[Y_{s}]_{p}
, as in
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\mathring{F}}
)
.

Claim 3.3.2
.

For every
e\in M^{s}
,
q
is consistent with
\varphi(xy;e)
if and only if, for every
(\mu_{i})_{i<n}\in\Lambda_{p}
, with
\forall_{\!p}xyF_{\mu_{i}}(x)\in\mathbf{k}_{F_{\lambda_{0}(x)}}
,
\exists_{\!p}xy\ \varphi(xy;e)\wedge y\in F_{\lambda_{0}}^{\cup}(x)\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i<n}F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
.

Proof.

Assume
q
is not consistent with
\varphi(xy;e)
, then, by compactness, there exists
(\mu_{i})_{i<m}\in\Lambda_{p}
such that
\mu_{i}<_{p}\mathcal{E}
and
\forall_{\!p}xy\ \varphi(xy;e)\wedge y\in F_{\lambda_{0}}^{\cup}(x)\to\bigvee_{i<m}y\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
. By the large ball property, we may assume
\forall_{\!p}xy\ F_{\mu_{i}}(x)\in\mathbf{k}_{F_{\lambda_{0}(x)}}
. Choosing a minimal
m
, we may also assume that,
\exists_{\!p}xy\ \varphi(xy;e)\wedge y\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(x)
. In particular,
\mu_{i}\in Y_{e}
.

By definition of
Y_{s}
, for every
\mu\in Y_{e}(M)
, we find
ac\models p
such that
\varphi(ac;e)
and
c\in F_{\mu}^{\cup}(a)\subseteq F_{\lambda_{0}}^{\cup}(a)
. So there is an
i
such that
c\in F_{\mu_{i}}^{\cup}(a)
. By irreducibility,
F_{\mu}(a)=F_{\mu_{i}}(a)
. It follows that
[Y_{e}]_{p}
is finite and thus
m\leqslant|[Y_{e}]_{p}|\leqslant n
.
∎

Since
q\vdash\varphi(xy;e)
if and only if
q
is not consistent with
\neg\varphi(xy;e)
,
3.3.2
allows us to conclude.
∎

We also need a better understanding of the interpretable set
[F_{\Lambda}]_{p}
. Note that it is, a priori,
\mathcal{L}(M)
-interpretable which is exactly the kind of sets elimination of imaginaries aims at describing. However, if
p
happens to be the restriction to
M
of a global
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable type, then
[F_{\Lambda}]_{p}
naturally embeds in an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-interpretable set. The goal of the following lemmas is to give (necessary) hypotheses under which any definable
p
verifies that condition. Valued vector spaces will play an important role:

Definition 3.3.3
.

Let
(K,\mathrm{v})
be a valued field and
V
be a
K
-vector space. A
valuation on
V
is a map
v:V\to X
where
X
is an ordered set with a maximal element
\infty
and an action
+
of
\Gamma
, respecting the order, such that:

•

v(0)=\infty
;

•

For all
x,y\in V
,
v(x+y)\geqslant\min\{v(x),v(y)\}
;

•

For all
a\in K
and
x\in V
,
v(a\cdot x)=\mathrm{v}(a)+v(x)
.

We say that a family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in V
is
separating
if for every
finite
I_{0}\subseteq I
and every
(a_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}\in K
,
v(\sum_{i\in I_{0}}a_{i}x_{i})=\min_{i\in I_{0}}(\mathrm{v}(a_{i})+v(x_{i}))
.

The following lemma owes much to Johnson’s computation of the canonical basis of
definable types in
\mathrm{ACVF}
,
cf.

[
Joh20
, Section 5.2]
. Let

\varphi_{d}(x;yz):=\mathrm{v}(\sum_{|I|<d}y_{I}x^{I})\geqslant\mathrm{v}(\sum_{|I|<d}z_{I}x^{I})
.

Proposition 3.3.4
.

Assume:

(
\mathbf{C}_{V}
)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
, every
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable valuation
v
on
\mathbf{K}^{n}
has a separating basis;

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)

T
has definably complete value group.

Then, for every
A=\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}
,
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable
p\in\mathrm{S}_{\varphi_{d}}(M)
and algebraic extension
K(M)\leqslant L
, any
q\in\mathrm{S}_{\varphi_{d}}(L)
, extending
p
and finitely satisfiable in
M\!
, is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable.

Proof.

For every field
F
with
K=\mathbf{K}(M)\leqslant F\leqslant L
, we define a valuation
v
on the
F
-vector space
V_{d}(F):=F[x]_{\leqslant d}
of polynomials in variables
x
over
F
of degree at most
d
by
v(P(x))\leqslant v(Q(x))
if
\mathrm{v}(P(x))\leqslant\mathrm{v}(Q(x))\in q
. The valuation
v
on
V_{d}(K)
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable. By hypothesis
(
\mathbf{C}_{V}
)
, it has a separating basis
(P_{i})_{i}\in V_{d}(K)
.

Claim 3.3.5
.

(P_{i})_{i}
is a separating basis of
V_{d}(L)
over
L
.

Proof.

We may assume that
K\leqslant L
is finite. By
[
Joh21
, Remark 2.7]
, the
valuation on
L
(interpreted in
K
) is then
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable and
hence, by hypothesis
(
\mathbf{C}_{V}
)
, also has a separating basis
(c_{j})_{j}\in L
over

K
. Let
b_{i}=\sum_{j}b_{i,j}c_{j}\in L
, where
b_{i,j}\in K
. If

v(\sum_{j}(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i})c_{j})>\min_{j}v(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i})+\mathrm{v}(c_{j})
, since
q
is finitely satisfiable in
M
, we have
v(\sum_{j}(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i}(a))c_{j})>\min_{j}v(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i}(a))+\mathrm{v}(c_{j})
for
some
a\in M
, contradicting that
(c_{j})_{j}
is separating over
K
. So

\displaystyle v(\sum_{i}b_{i}P_{i})

\displaystyle=v(\sum_{j}(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i})c_{j})

\displaystyle=\min_{j}v(\sum_{i}b_{i,j}P_{i})+\mathrm{v}(c_{j})

\displaystyle=\min_{i,j}\mathrm{v}(b_{i,j})+\mathrm{v}(c_{j})+v(P_{i})

\displaystyle\leqslant\min_{i}\mathrm{v}(b_{i})+v(P_{i})

\displaystyle\leqslant v(\sum_{i}b_{i}P_{i})\qed

We now define the
L
-Archimedean equivalence on
v(V_{d}(L))
:
v(P)\sim_{L}^{\infty}v(Q)
holds if there exists
c\in L^{\times}
, with
\mathrm{v}(x)\geqslant 0
, such that
-\mathrm{v}(c)+v(P)\leqslant v(Q)\leqslant\mathrm{v}(c)+v(P)
. One can check
that
|v(V_{d}(L))/\sim_{L}^{\infty}|\leqslant|v(V_{d}(L))/\mathrm{v}(L)|\leqslant\dim_{L}(V_{d}(L))+1<\infty
. We also define the
L
-infinitesimal
equivalence on
v(V_{d}(L))
:
v(P)\sim_{L}^{0}v(Q)
holds if for every
\gamma\in\mathrm{v}(L)_{>0}
we have
-\gamma+v(P)<v(Q)<\gamma+v(P)
. Note that
two elements of the same
\mathrm{v}(L)
-orbit cannot be
\sim^{0}_{L}
-equivalent
unless they are equal. It follows that
\sim_{L}^{0}
-classes are finite.

Let
C
be any
K
-Archimedean class and
\overline{C}
denote its upwards
closure. Then
V_{C}:=v^{-1}(\overline{C})\leqslant V_{d}(K)
is an

\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable sub-
K
-vector space.

Claim 3.3.6

(
[
Joh20
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

V_{C}
has a basis of elements in
A
.

Proof.

Some coordinate projection
V_{C}\subseteq K^{l}\to K^{m}
restricts to an
isomorphism on
V_{C}
. The preimage of the standard basis of
K^{m}
then has
the required properties.
∎

Let
C_{0}
be the successor of
C
in
V_{d}(K)/\sim_{K}^{\infty}
. Since

V_{C_{0}}\subset V_{C}
, any basis of
V_{C}
has an element outside
V_{C_{0}}
.
In particular
(V_{C}\smallsetminus V_{C_{0}})(A)\neq\emptyset
and we find

\gamma_{C}\in C(A)\neq\emptyset
. Then the whole (finite)
K
-infinitesimal
class of
\gamma_{C}
is in
A
. Let
i
be such that
v(P_{i})\in C
. By

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)
, the set
\{\gamma\in\mathrm{v}(K):\gamma+v(P_{i})\leqslant\gamma_{C}\}
has a
supremum
\gamma_{i}
. Multiplying
P_{i}
by some constant
c\in K
with

\mathrm{v}(c)=-\gamma_{i}
, we may assume that
\gamma_{i}=0
in which case

v(P_{i})
is
K
-infinitesimally close to
\gamma_{C}\in A
. Since the

K
-infinitesimal class of
\gamma_{C}
is finite, it follows that
v(P_{i})

is also in
A
. Since every
\mathrm{v}(K)
-orbit is contained in some

K
-Archimedean class, we now have that for any
i
,
v(P_{i})\in A
and
for any
j
, if
v(P_{i})\sim_{K}^{\infty}v(P_{j})
, then
v(P_{i})\sim_{K}^{0}v(P_{j})
.

Note that, since
\mathrm{v}(L)
is in the convex hull of
\mathrm{v}(K)
,
\sim_{L}^{\infty}
extends
\sim_{K}^{\infty}
. Also, if
v(K)
is dense, then
\sim_{L}^{0}
extends
\sim_{K}^{0}
. However, if
\mathrm{v}(K)
is discrete then
\sim_{K}^{0}
reduces to equality. In particular, we also have that, if
v(P_{i})\sim_{L}^{\infty}v(P_{j})
, then
v(P_{i})\sim_{L}^{0}v(P_{j})
.

For every
i
,
d
, let
M_{i,d}(L)=\{P\in V_{d}(L):v(P)\geqslant v(P_{i})\}
. Note that, by
3.3.5
,
\sum\lambda_{j}P_{j}\in M_{i,d}(L)
if and only if:

•

\lambda_{j}=0
for every
j
with
v(P_{j})<v(P_{i})
and
v(P_{j})\nsim_{K}^{\infty}v(P_{i})
;

•

\lambda_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}
for
j
with
v(P_{j})<v(P_{i})
and
v(P_{j})\sim_{K}^{0}v(P_{i})
;

•

\lambda_{j}\in\mathcal{O}
for
j
with
v(P_{i})\leqslant v(P_{j})
and
v(P_{j})\sim_{K}^{0}v(P_{i})
.

So
M_{i,d}
is (quantifier free)
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable. Since
q
is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bigcup_{i,d}\ulcorner M_{i,d}\urcorner)
-definable, it is indeed
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable.
∎

If
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}(M)
the existence (and uniqueness) of such a
q
follows, on general grounds, from the finite satisfiability of
p
:

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}(M)
be finitely satisfiable in
M
. Then any two realisations of
p
have the same
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(M))
-type. In particular, the unique extension of
p
to
\mathrm{acl}_{0}(M)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
.

Proof.

Fix any
c\in\mathrm{acl}_{0}(M)
,
\varphi(xy)
an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formula and
\psi(y)
an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-formula that algebrises
c
. Then
\forall y\ [\psi(y)\to(\varphi(x_{1}y)\leftrightarrow\varphi(x_{2}y)
)] defines an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable equivalence relation with finitely many classes. Then the
E
-class of any
a\in N\succcurlyeq M
realising
p
has an element
e\in M
. It follows that
p(x)\vdash xEe
. In particular
p(x)\vdash\varphi(xc)
whenever
\varphi(ec)
holds.

Let
q
be the unique extension of
p
to
\mathrm{acl}_{0}(M)
. Then
p\vdash q
and hence
q
is finitely satisfiable in
M
.
∎

Following
[
Bau82
]
, we can prove that
(
\mathbf{C}_{V}
)
follows from definable spherical completeness. Up to definability, this is a standard result. But we include its proof, on the one hand, for the sake of completeness, and, on the other, to show that the proof can indeed been done definably.

Lemma 3.3.8
.

Assume:

(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{B}}
)

T
is definably spherically complete : any
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable chain of balls has a non-empty intersection.

Then any (finite dimensional)
\mathcal{L}(M)
-interpretable valued
\mathbf{K}
-vector space
(V,v)
has a separating basis.

Proof.

Let us proceed by induction on
n+1:=\dim(V)
. In particular, we may assume that we have found a separating family
(y_{i})_{0\leqslant i<n}\in V
.

Claim 3.3.9
.

For every
x\in V
,
\{v(x-\lambda y):\lambda\in\mathbf{K}^{n}\}
has a maximal element.

Proof.

For every
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbf{K}^{n}
, we have
v(x-\mu y)\geqslant v(x-\lambda y)=:\gamma
if and only if
\min_{i}\{\mathrm{v}(\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i})+v(y_{i})\}=v((\mu-\lambda)y)\geqslant\gamma
. For every
i<n
and
\lambda\in\mathbf{K}^{n}
, let
B_{i,\lambda}:=\{\mu\in\mathbf{K}:v(x-\lambda_{\neq i}y_{\neq i}-\mu y_{i})\geqslant v(x-\lambda y)\}=\{\mu\in\mathbf{K}:\mathrm{v}(\mu-\lambda_{i})+v(y_{i})\geqslant v(x-\lambda y)\}
. They form a chain for inclusion.

If there is a minimal
B_{i,\lambda_{0}}
, pick any
\lambda_{i}\in B_{i,\lambda_{0}}
. If there is no minimal
B_{i,\lambda}
, for every
\gamma\in\mathrm{v}(\mathbf{K})
, let
b_{i,\gamma}
be the closed ball of radius
\gamma
containing some
B_{i,\lambda}
, if it exists, or
\mathbf{K}
otherwise. Since the chain of
B_{i,\lambda}
does not have a minimal element, any
B_{i,\lambda}
contains a
b_{i,\gamma}
that itself contains a
B_{i,\mu}
. By definable spherical completeness, we find
\lambda_{i}\in\bigcap_{\gamma}b_{i,\gamma}=\bigcap_{\lambda}B_{i,\lambda}
. Then
\lambda_{i}
has the property that, for any
\mu\in\mathbf{K}^{n}
,
v(x-\mu y)\leqslant v(x-\mu_{\neq i}y_{\neq_{i}}-\lambda_{i}y_{i})
. It follows that
v(x-\lambda y)
is maximal.
∎

Let
x
be linearly independent from the
y_{i}
. By the
3.3.9
, we may assume that
v(x)=\max\{v(x-\lambda y):\lambda\in\mathbf{K}^{n}\}
. Then, for every
\mu\in\mathbf{K}
and
\lambda\in\mathbf{K}^{n}
, we have
v(\mu x+\lambda y)\leqslant v(\mu x)
and thus,
v(\mu x+\lambda y)=\min\{v(\mu x),v(\lambda y)\}=\min_{i}\{v(\mu x),v(\lambda_{i}y_{i})\}
.
∎

Remark 3.3.10
.

Note that given any basis of
V
, in the above lemma, we actually construct a
separating basis whose base change is upper triangular.

3.4.
Counting germs

The last ingredient in this section is to reduce the (seemingly horrendous) hypotheses
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\mathring{F}}
)
and
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\overline{F}}
)
to something more tractable. We start by generalizing
[
Rid19
, Section 7]
. Let
M\models T
,
M_{0}:=M^{\mathrm{a}}\models T_{0}
and
Q
be either
\mathbf{\Gamma}
or
\mathbf{k}
.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Let
(f_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{n}\to Q
be an
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable family and
c\in\mathbf{K}(N_{0})^{n}
, where
N_{0}\succcurlyeq M_{0}
. There exists an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable family
(g_{\rho})_{\rho\in R}:\mathbf{K}^{n}\to Q^{[<\infty]}
, with
R\subseteq Q^{m}
, such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda(M_{0})
, there exists
\rho\in R(M_{0})
with
f_{\lambda}(c)\in g_{\rho}(c)
.

In particular, if
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}(M_{0})
is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable, there is a
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable finite-to-one map
\{[f_{\lambda}]_{p}:\lambda\in\Lambda(M_{0})\}\to Q^{m}
, for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Proof.

We start with the non-uniform version of the result:

Claim 3.4.2
.

For every
N_{0}\models\mathrm{ACVF}
,
A\leqslant\mathbf{K}(N_{0})
and finite tuple
c\in\mathbf{K}(N_{0})
, there exists a finite tuple
a\in A
, such that
Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Ac))\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(A)ac)
.

Proof.

If
|c|=1
, let
a_{0}\in\mathbf{K}(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(A))
be such that
\mathrm{v}(c-a_{0})

is maximal — if it exists, otherwise the extension
A\leqslant A(c)
is immediate
and we take
a_{0}=0
. Then
\mathbf{RV}(A(c))\subseteq\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(\mathrm{rv}(A)\mathrm{rv}(c-a_{0}))
.
It follows that
Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Ac))=\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(A(c)))\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(A))ca_{0})=\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(A)ca_{0})
. If
a\in A
is such that

a_{0}\in\mathrm{acl}_{0}(a)
, we indeed have
Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Ac))\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(A)ac)
.

If
c=de
with
|e|=1
, we proceed by induction:

Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Ade))\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Ad))be)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Q(A)acbe),

with
a,b\in A
.
∎

By
3.4.2
, and compactness in a saturated model of the pair

(N_{0},M_{0})
, there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M_{0})
-definable
g
as above. The
union of its conjugates over
M
has the same properties and is

\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable.

Now, if
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}(M_{0})
is
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable, then for any

\lambda\in\Lambda(M_{0})
, let
Y_{\lambda}:=\{\rho:\forall_{\!p}{x}\ f_{\lambda}(x)\in g_{\rho}(x)\}
and
h([f_{\lambda}]_{p}):=\ulcorner Y_{\lambda}\urcorner\in Q^{m}
. Note that
p(x)\models f_{\mu}(x)\in\bigcap_{\rho\in Y_{\mu}}g_{\rho}(x)
which is a finite set. It follows that there are at most
finitely many germs
[f_{\mu}]_{p}
associated to a given
Y_{\lambda}
, in
other words,
h
is finite-to-one.
∎

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Assume:

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})

For any
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable
(Y_{z})_{z}\subseteq\mathbf{k}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that, for all
z
,
|Y_{z}|<\infty
implies
|Y_{z}|\leqslant n
;

Then, for every
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable
p\in\mathrm{S}^{0}_{x}(M_{0})
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
,
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\mathring{F}}
)
holds, uniformly in
\lambda
.

Proof.

The core of the proof is the following almost internality result:

Claim 3.4.4
.

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda(M)
, there exists an

\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable finite-to-one map
g_{\lambda}:X_{\lambda}:=\{[F_{\mu}]_{p}:\mu\in\Lambda(M_{0})
and
p(x)\vdash

“
F_{\mu}(x)
are
maximal open balls of
F_{\lambda}(x)
”
\}\to\mathbf{k}^{m}
, for some

m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Proof.

Let
c\models p
. In
M(c)^{\mathrm{a}}\models\mathrm{ACVF}
, any closed ball of

F_{\lambda}(c)
has at least two (infinitely many, in fact) distinct maximal
open subballs. So there exists
G_{i}(c)\in\mathbf{B}^{[<\infty]}
, for
i:=1,2
, two
\mathcal{L}_{0}(Mc)
-definable sets picking at least one maximal open balls
in each of the ball of
F_{\lambda}(c)
and such that
G_{1}(c)\cap G_{2}(c)=\emptyset
. For every
\mu
with
[F_{\mu}]_{p}\in X_{\lambda}
, let

f_{\mu}(x):=\{(b-b_{1})/(b_{2}-b_{1}):b\in F_{\mu}(x),\ b_{i}\in G_{i}(x)
and

b,b_{1},b_{2}
are in the same ball of
F_{\lambda}(x)\}\in\mathbf{k}^{[<\infty]}
.
Note that
F_{\mu}(c)\in\mathrm{acl}_{0}(f_{\mu}(c)G_{1}(c)G_{2}(c))
. Using symmetric
functions, we identify
\mathbf{k}^{[<\infty]}
with some
\mathbf{k}^{n}
.

By
Lemma

3.4.1
, we find an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable finite to one map
h:\{[F_{\mu}]_{p}:[F_{\mu}]_{p}\in X_{\lambda}\}\to\mathbf{k}^{m}
. Then
[F_{\mu}]_{p}\in\mathrm{acl}_{0}([G_{1}]_{p}[G_{1}]_{p}[f_{\mu}]_{p})\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{0}(Mh(\mu))
and
h(\mu)\in\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(M[f_{\mu}]_{p})\subseteq\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(M[F_{\mu}]_{p})
.
∎

Since
\mathbf{k}(\mathrm{dcl}_{0}(M))
is the perfect closure of
\mathbf{k}(M)
, by compactness, composing with a power of the Frobenius automorphism, we may assume that
g_{\lambda}(X_{\lambda}(M))\subseteq\mathbf{k}(M)
and that
g_{\lambda}
in uniform in
\lambda
. The (uniform) bound in
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\mathring{F}}
)
now follows from
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Assume
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
and:

(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})

For any
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable
(Y_{z})_{z}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
such that, for all
z
,
|Y_{z}|<\infty
implies
|Y_{z}|\leqslant n
.

Then for every
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable
p\in\mathrm{S}_{x}^{0}(M_{0})
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-definable
(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}:\mathbf{K}^{x}\to\mathbf{B}^{[r]}
, and
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable
(Y_{z})_{z}\subseteq[F_{\Lambda}]_{p}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
|Y_{z}|<\infty
implies
|Y_{z}|\leqslant n
.

In particular,
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{p,\overline{F}}
)
holds.

Proof.

Let
r_{\lambda}(x):=\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda}(x))
. By
Lemma

3.4.1
, there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
-definable finite-to-one map
g:[r_{\Lambda}]_{p}\to\Gamma^{m}
. Composing by division by a fixed integer, we may assume that
g([{r_{\Lambda}}]_{p}(M))\subseteq\Gamma(M)
. It now follows that there is a bound on finite
\{[r_{\lambda}]_{p}:[F_{\lambda}]_{p}\in Y_{z}\}
. So, cutting each
Y_{z}
in finitely many pieces (and getting rid of the infinite ones), we may assume that
[\mathrm{rad}(F_{\lambda})]_{p}
is constant and the balls are of the same type, as
[F_{\lambda}]_{p}
ranges through
Y_{z}
. Similarly, we may assume that the set of distances between balls in
F_{\lambda}(x)
and
F_{\mu}(x)
, with
[F_{\lambda}]_{p},[F_{\lambda}]_{p}\in Y_{z}
has size bounded by some integer
k
. We now proceed by induction on
k
.

Let
\gamma_{z}(x)
be the smallest such distance,
G_{\lambda,z}(x)
be the set of closed balls of radius
\gamma_{z}(x)
around
F_{\lambda}(x)
and
Z_{z}:=\{[G_{\lambda,z}]_{p}:[F_{\lambda}]_{p}\in Y_{z}\}
. Then the set of distances between balls in
Z_{z}
has size at most
k-1
and we find a bound by induction. In particular, removing some more infinite
Y_{z}
, we find an
H_{z}:\mathbf{K}^{n}\to\mathbf{B}^{[<\infty]}
such that every maximal open ball of
H_{z}(x)
contains at most one ball of
F_{\lambda}(x)
as
[F_{\lambda}]_{p}
varies through
Y_{z}
. The bound now follows from
Lemma

3.4.3
.
∎

3.5.
The higher arity case

We can now proceed with the induction:

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Assume
(
\mathbf{C}_{V}
)
,
(
\mathbf{C}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
)
,
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{k}})
and
(
\mathbf{E}^{\infty}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
. Let
X\subseteq\mathbf{K}^{x}
be strict pro-
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable, where
A=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\subseteq M^{\mathrm{eq}}
and
x
is countable. Let
\Delta(x;t)
be a finite set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas,
p\in\mathrm{S}_{x}^{\Delta}(M)
be
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
and consistent with
X
and
z\subseteq x
. Then, there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable
q\in\mathrm{S}^{0}_{z}(M_{0})
such that
{\left.q\right|_{M}}
is consistent with
p
and
X
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
|z|
. In particular, we may assume that for any set
\Delta(x;t)
of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas,
p\in\mathrm{S}_{x}^{\Delta}(M)
which is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
and finite strictly smaller
w\subset z
,
{\left.p\right|_{w}}
can be extended to an
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbfcal{G}(A))
-definable
q\in\mathrm{S}^{0}_{w}(M_{0})
.

Claim 3.5.2
.

Let
\Delta(x;t)
be a finite set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas,
p\in\mathrm{S}_{x}^{\Delta}(M)
be
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
and consistent with
X
, finite
z\subseteq x
and
\Phi(z;s)
be a finite set of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-formulas. Then, there exists a finite set
\Theta(z;t)
containing
\Phi
and
q\in\mathrm{S}^{\Delta,\Theta}_{x}(M)
which is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-quantifiable over
\mathcal{L}
and consistent with
p
and
X
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
|z|
. Assume
z=wy
with
|y|=1
(where
w
might be the empty tuple). By
Proposition

3.2.8
